--- SENTENCE 1 ---
# **Long term monitoring of Fagatele Bay National Marine Sanctuary, Tutuila Island, American Samoa: results of surveys conducted in 2004, including a re-survey of the historic Aua Transect.

--- SENTENCE 2 ---
**

Alison Green Karen Miller Craig Mundy

13 July, 2005

Report prepared for:

**U.S. Department of Commerce American Samoa Government**  National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration Department of Commerce National Ocean Service Environment Division Office of National Marine Sanctuaries

!

--- SENTENCE 3 ---
[](_page_0_Picture_7.jpeg)

#### **EXECUTIVE SUMMARY**

- Long-term monitoring of the fish and coral communities in Fagatele Bay National Marine Sanctuary and at other sites around Tutuila Island has been under way since the late 1970s and early 1980s, respectively.

--- SENTENCE 4 ---
- Permanent transects (sites) in Fagatele Bay were re-surveyed in September 2004 to assess changes in coral and fish communities through time.

--- SENTENCE 5 ---
- New coral survey methods were implemented in 2004.

--- SENTENCE 6 ---
We used belt transects rather than the point-quarter method used previously.

--- SENTENCE 7 ---
This allowed us to survey almost three times as many corals than in previous surveys within a similar period of time.

--- SENTENCE 8 ---
The new method will provide more detailed information for monitoring the health of the corals in Fagatele Bay and a better comparison with the Archipelago survey dataset.

--- SENTENCE 9 ---
- Coral communities in Fagatele Bay generally show improved condition since the previous survey in 2001.

--- SENTENCE 10 ---
The coral communities on the reef slope have increased in terms of the number of colonies, colony size and coral cover, although communities are still dominated by fast growing, opportunistic species.

--- SENTENCE 11 ---
Coral communities on the reef flat appear to be recovering from the mass die-off in 1998 associated with coral bleaching.

--- SENTENCE 12 ---
- The results from the 2004 survey indicate that the coral communities in Fagatele Bay continue to recover from the major disturbances of the last few decades.

--- SENTENCE 13 ---
There appears to have been little effect of the recent hurricane (Heta) on the corals in Fagatele Bay.

--- SENTENCE 14 ---
- Large monospecific stands of a few coral species have been identified as key conservation features of Fagatele Bay.

--- SENTENCE 15 ---
These features are likely to be many hundreds of years old and future monitoring to record the status of these corals is recommended.

--- SENTENCE 16 ---
- There was an apparent decline in fish abundance in Fagatele Bay since 2001, which was consistent across species and genera.

--- SENTENCE 17 ---
This is most likely associated with seasonal variation in abundance, since surveys were conducted in September 2004 rather than during the recruitment season in March, as was the case in previous surveys.

--- SENTENCE 18 ---
The surveys were moved to late summer to avoid this recruitment season, which makes fish counting more difficult because of the large number of ephemeral juveniles at that time.

--- SENTENCE 19 ---
The addition of size estimates to the methods has allowed for the interpretation of this confounding effect using the size structure of the fish populations.

--- SENTENCE 20 ---
- Scarids (parrotfishes) are the only fish family that appears to have increased in abundance and size in Fagatele Bay since 2001.

--- SENTENCE 21 ---
This may be a result of the ban on SCUBA fishing which was implemented in 2001 to minimise damaging effects on fish communities and particularly on scarid populations.

--- SENTENCE 22 ---
However, there are still signs of the effects of fishing in the Sanctuary.

--- SENTENCE 23 ---
- There was a decline in the fish populations at the three historic transects surveyed around Tutuila (Fagatele Bay, Sita Bay and Cape Larsen).

--- SENTENCE 24 ---
This may be related to seasonal variation in sampling, but may also reflect the effects of overfishing and Hurricane Heta in January 2004, which particularly affected northern Tutuila (where two transects are located).

--- SENTENCE 25 ---
- Coral communities continue to improve along the Aua Transect on the reef flat in Pago Pago Harbour.

--- SENTENCE 26 ---
Abundance and percent cover of corals continue to increase, although species richness remains low.

--- SENTENCE 27 ---
This may be the result of improved management to reduce pollution in the harbour.

--- SENTENCE 28 ---
- Coral populations on the reef slopes in Pago Pago Harbour are also showing some signs of recovery.

--- SENTENCE 29 ---
There is evidence of new coral recruitment at Rainmaker, although there has been a mysterious decline in coral populations on the reef slope at Aua.

#### **INTRODUCTION**

The coral reefs of American Samoa have suffered many destructive impacts in the last few decades, including a major outbreak of the corallivorous starfish *Acanthaster planci,* several devastating hurricanes, a period of high water temperature, and exposure during unusually low tides (Birkeland et al. 1996, 2003, 2004, Green 1996, 1997, 2002, Green et al. 1999).

--- SENTENCE 41 ---
The reefs in some locations, such as Pago Pago Harbour, have also been subjected to human impacts including sedimentation, eutrophication, pollution and overfishing (Birkeland et al. 1996, 2003, 2004, Green 1996, 1997, 2002, Green et al. 1997).

--- SENTENCE 42 ---
As a direct result of these disturbances, the reefs of Fagatele Bay National Marine Sanctuary (FBNMS) and other locations around Tutuila Island have undergone some major changes in the last 25 years (Birkeland et al. 1996, 2003, Green et al. 1997).

--- SENTENCE 43 ---
Of particular concern have been the physical and biological changes to the coral communities in some locations.

--- SENTENCE 44 ---
Coral communities provide important habitat for reef fishes and changes in their condition may have important consequences for the associated fish fauna.

--- SENTENCE 45 ---
The coral reef communities of Fagatele Bay and other sites around Tutuila have been the subjects of a long term monitoring program since the late 1970s, supported by the Fagatele Bay National Marine Sanctuary Program.

--- SENTENCE 46 ---
The purpose of the ongoing monitoring of reef communities is to document the recovery, or further decline, of coral and fish communities in Fagatele Bay, and elsewhere around Tutuila, and to determine if reef communities have changed substantially since the combined effects of *A. planci* outbreaks and hurricanes that devastated American Samoa reefs in the late 80's.

--- SENTENCE 47 ---
The results of the monitoring program have been used to describe the changes in the coral and fish communities of American Samoa during the 23 years from 1978-2001 (Birkeland et al. 1987, 1994, 1996, 2003, 2004; Green et al. 1999).

--- SENTENCE 48 ---
This report documents the results from surveys in Fagatele Bay National Marine Sanctuary conducted in September 2004, and includes results from a re-survey of the historic Aua Transect located in Pago Pago Harbour, which was first surveyed in 1924 (Mayor 1924).

#### **METHODS**

#### **Survey techniques – Fagatele Bay National Marine Sanctuary**

#### *Corals*

Coral communities in Fagatele Bay have been surveyed previously using the point-quarter method as described in Birkeland et al. 1987.

--- SENTENCE 49 ---
Following the recommendations of the previous report on the status of the reefs in Fagatele Bay (Birkeland et al. 2004), for the 2004 surveys we switched to using belt transects to collect data on coral communities.

--- SENTENCE 50 ---
This alternate method allows the determination of abundance, size, and percent cover of corals in the bay, but samples considerably more corals resulting in a more detailed data set that would be collected using the point-quarter method.

--- SENTENCE 51 ---
The belt-transect method is also direct comparable with the archipelagowide survey (Mundy 1996, Fisk & Birkeland 2002).

--- SENTENCE 52 ---
We recorded all corals whose centre occurred in a 20 x 0.5m belt transect laid perpendicular to the six permanent transect lines in FBNMS (Transects 1-6, Figure 1).

--- SENTENCE 53 ---
Each coral was identified to species, and categorised into one of seven size classes based on the maximum diameter of the colony; <5cm, 5-10cm, 10-20cm, 20-40cm, 40-80cm, 80-160cm and >160cm.

--- SENTENCE 54 ---
At Sites 2 to 5, we collected data at 3, 6, 9 and 12m depths on the reef slope.

--- SENTENCE 55 ---
Due to rough weather, we were only able to sample from depths of 9 and 12m on the more exposed locations at Sites 1 and 6.

--- SENTENCE 56 ---
Two belt-transects were also surveyed on the reef flat, at locations B and C (Figure 1).

--- SENTENCE 57 ---
Coral data were collected in September 2004 by Craig Mundy and Karen Miller.

#### *Fishes*

Fish communities were surveyed in FBNMS using the same underwater visual census technique that has been used previously (ie. fixed length transects, 30 x 2m).

--- SENTENCE 58 ---
This method is described in detail elsewhere (Birkeland et al. 1987, 1994).

--- SENTENCE 59 ---
In addition to recording fish species and abundance on each transect as in all previous surveys, fish sizes have also been recorded since 1998.

--- SENTENCE 60 ---
Fish were surveyed at five depths (3, 6, 9, 12 and 18m) on the reef slope at Sites 2 to 5, and at two depths (9 and 12m) at Sites 1 and 6.

--- SENTENCE 61 ---
Due to rough weather, fish were not counted on the reef flat or shallower than 9m at transects 1 and 6.

--- SENTENCE 62 ---
Fish surveys were conducted in September 2004 by Alison Green.

#### *Benthic macroinvertebrates and fish habitat*

In line with the archipelago-wide surveys of the reefs of American Samoa (Green 1996, 2002), we have included counts of key macroinvertebrates and benthic species in the FBNMS surveys to provide an indication of the condition of fish habitat in the bay.

--- SENTENCE 63 ---
Point counts were used to assess fish habitat species, whereby 48 points were sampled along each 30 x 2m fish transect.

--- SENTENCE 64 ---
At every 2 metres along the tape from 0m to 30m, three points were sampled (one directly under the tape and one approximately 1m either side of the tape).

--- SENTENCE 65 ---
The benthos under each point was recorded as one of 23 categories within four key taxonomic groups:

- Corals (branching, digitate, encrusting, foliaceous, massive, mushroom, plate);
- Macroalgae (encrusting, blue green, *Halimeda*, macroalgae, pink coralline algae);
- Miscellaneous (ascidian, clam, hydrozoan, soft coral, sponge, urchin, zooanthid); and
- Non-living (crevice/hole, reef matrix, rubble, sand).

--- SENTENCE 66 ---
#### *Relocation of Permanent Transect Sites in Fagatele Bay*

--- SENTENCE 67 ---
Unfortunately, the GPS readings that were taken at each of the six permanent transect sites in FBNMS in 1995 have been misplaced.

--- SENTENCE 68 ---
For the 2004 survey, we relocated permanent transects according to the habitat and reef descriptions outlined in Birkeland et al. 1987, and from personal notes taken on previous surveys.

--- SENTENCE 69 ---
We also took GPS readings at the 12m point at each site, as well as other key locations, to facilitate relocation of the sites in future surveys.

--- SENTENCE 70 ---
Descriptions of geographic features used to relocate the sites and GPS coordinates are provided in Appendix 1.

!

--- SENTENCE 71 ---
Map of Fagatele Bay showing six permanent transects (T<sup>1</sup> – T6 ) and the approximate locations of the coral and fish surveys at each depth along each transect.

--- SENTENCE 72 ---
The location of three reef flat sites (A-C) is also indicated.

#### **Survey techniques – Pago Pago Harbour**

To provide comparisons between the coral reef communities in Fagatele Bay and elsewhere around Tutuila, corals and fish have been surveyed in up to 13 other sites around the island in conjunction with the FBNMS survey (see Birkeland et al. 1987, 1994, 1996, 2003, 2004).

--- SENTENCE 73 ---
However, surveys at sites outside Fagatele Bay have been largely superceded by the archipelagowide surveys that commenced in 1996 and which were last surveyed in 2002 (Mundy 1996, Fisk & Birkeland 2002, Green 2002).

--- SENTENCE 74 ---
Hence most of the sites outside of FBNMS that have been surveyed in previous years have not been included in the 2004 survey.

--- SENTENCE 75 ---
The two sites in Pago Pago Harbour (Rainmaker Hotel and Aua), which provide useful information for management (Birkeland et al 2004), were resurveyed in 2004 in conjunction with the Fagatele Bay surveys.

--- SENTENCE 76 ---
At these two sites, corals were surveyed at 3m and 6m depths on the reef slope.

--- SENTENCE 77 ---
Survey methods were as described above, with corals surveyed in a single 20 x 0.5m belt transect at each depth and with each colony identified to species and categorised into one of seven size classes.

--- SENTENCE 78 ---
GPS co-ordinates at both sites were also recorded to facilitate future relocation of the survey locations (Appendix 1).

#### **Survey techniques – The Aua Transect**

Corals and macroinvertebrates along the historic Aua Transect (first surveyed by Mayor 1924) were also surveyed in 2004.

--- SENTENCE 79 ---
The transect line was relocated by carefully following the description of the location of the transect in Mayor (1924) and Dahl and Lamberts (1977).

--- SENTENCE 80 ---
Essentially, the transect followed a bearing of 39.5°W from a well-cemented block near the reef crest and above the general contour of the reef.

--- SENTENCE 81 ---
Sampling distances were measured back from the reef crest, assuming the crest was 271m from shore (as measured in previous surveys, Green et al. 1997).

--- SENTENCE 82 ---
The distances from shore that were surveyed in 2004 were 259m, 247m, 233m, 213m, 183, 160, 140, 122 and 91.

--- SENTENCE 83 ---
Corals were measured (maximum diameter to the nearest cm) and key macroinvertebrates were counted using a number of replicated  $0.25\text{m}^2$  quadrats located hapahazardly within a 7m x 7m square at each distance from shore.

--- SENTENCE 84 ---
For the distances between 233m and 259m from shore, corals were counted in 20 quadrats.

--- SENTENCE 85 ---
This was less than had been sampled in each area in previous years (Green et al.

--- SENTENCE 86 ---
1997, Birkeland & Belliaveau 2000) due to concerns of oversampling i.e. if sampling randomly to estimate abundance, it is considered unwise to sample more than 10% of an area (Krebs 1999).

--- SENTENCE 87 ---
As the overall sampling area at each distance from shore was  $49\text{m}^2$ ,  $20 \times 0.25\text{m}^2$  quadrats represents around 10% of the area.

--- SENTENCE 88 ---
Forty quadrats were sampled in those areas where corals were less abundant (91-213m from shore) to ensure species diversity was adequately recorded.

--- SENTENCE 89 ---
For the area < 91m from shore, now part of a dredge channel (Green et al. 1997), snorkel swims were conducted in the general area to determine if any corals were present.

--- SENTENCE 90 ---
Corals were surveyed by Craig Mundy and Karen Miller.

#### Survey techniques – Historic fish transects around Tutuila

Fish communities have been surveyed in a single transect on the reef slope at each of three sites (Fagatele Bay (12m), Sita Bay (5-6m) and Cape Larsen (8-9m), since 1977.

--- SENTENCE 91 ---
These surveys used a single transect at each site, and were initially surveyed as part of an assessment of the impacts of the crown-of-thorns starfish, *Acanthaster planci* (see Birkeland et al. 1987, 2003).

--- SENTENCE 92 ---
We have continued to survey them as part of the Fagatele Bay surveys to follow long term trends in fish communities at each of the sites, and because they form the oldest continuous survey data from American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 93 ---
Larger transects (100m x 2 m) have used in this part of the study, because they were originally designed as part of a different project (see Wass 1982), but due to the different depths and locations of sampling at each site, the data are not directly comparable among the three sites.

--- SENTENCE 94 ---
In the 2004 re-survey of these transects, we modified the survey method to include three replicate 30m x 2m transects rather than a continuous 100m x 2m transect.

--- SENTENCE 95 ---
This allowed us to survey a similar area, while introducing replication into the survey design.

--- SENTENCE 96 ---
The exact location of the start of each of the transects is described in Birkeland et al. (1987), and GPS co-ordinates were recorded at each site in 2004 to facilitate relocation (Appendix 1).

--- SENTENCE 97 ---
Fishes were surveyed by Alison Green.

!

--- SENTENCE 98 ---
Map of Tutuila showing the approximate location of the two coral survey sites in Pago Pago Harbour (Rainmaker and Aua (including the Aua transect)), and the location of the three historic fish transects around the island.

### **Data analysis**

Coral data from Fagatele Bay and the two sites in Pago Pago Harbour were summarised to determine the abundance and percent cover of each species at each depth.

--- SENTENCE 99 ---
Percent cover was calculated according to Mundy (1996), and based on the median colony size within each size class.

--- SENTENCE 100 ---
Fish data from Fagatele Bay, and the three historic fish transects (Fagatele Bay, Cape Larsen and Sita Bay) were summarised to determine the relative abundance and species richness at each site and each depth.

--- SENTENCE 101 ---
Fish data from the historic transects were also converted to density estimates (no. individuals per hectare) to make the data among years more comparable (due to the change in sampling area in the 2004 surveys), and to match international standards for fish survey data.

--- SENTENCE 102 ---
Recent changes in coral and fish communities in Fagatele Bay, Pago Pago Harbour and the other sites around Tutuila were described by comparing trends between the previous survey (conducted in 2001, Birkeland et al. 2004) and results from the current survey.

--- SENTENCE 103 ---
In addition, changes in coral and fish communities in Fagatele Bay over the last 25 years were examined by comparing the 2004 data with results from surveys prior to 2001, as described in Birkeland et al. (2003).

--- SENTENCE 104 ---
Fish size data in Fagatele Bay is only available for the 2001 and 2004 surveys.

--- SENTENCE 105 ---
These data were summarised at the family level to determine the size-frequency of four important fisheries groups (Acanthurids, Scarids, Lethrinids and Serranids; see Green 2002 for further details) and compared between years to determine if there were any recent changes in the communities.

--- SENTENCE 106 ---
We also compared size frequency data between years for two key fisheries species; *Cephalopholis argus* (Family Serranidae) and *Ctenochaetus striatus* (Family Acanthuridae).

--- SENTENCE 107 ---
Macroinvertebrate and fish habitat data were summarised to determine the percentage of all points represented by each of the 24 categories at each site and at each depth.

--- SENTENCE 108 ---
The coral data collected from the Aua Transect was summarised to determine the abundance of each species (as the number of colonies per square metre) at each distance from shore, as well as the average size of colonies for each species.

--- SENTENCE 109 ---
To monitor changes through time, we compared the overall abundance and percent cover at each distance among surveys undertaken from 1995 to 2004.

--- SENTENCE 110 ---
Trends in the abundance of the three groups of macro invertebrates were also examined over time.

#### **RESULTS**

#### **Fagatele Bay National Marine Sanctuary**

# *Corals in Fagatele Bay – 2004*

A total of 3385 corals colonies belonging to 101 species was recorded during surveys in Fagatele Bay in 2004.

--- SENTENCE 111 ---
The most common coral in the bay (547 colonies) was *Galaxea fascicularis*.

--- SENTENCE 112 ---
Four other species were common and represented by >200 colonies including *Montipora grisea* (411 colonies), *Pavona divaricata* (311 colonies), *Montipora venosa* (261 colonies) and *Porites rus* (244 colonies).

--- SENTENCE 113 ---
Most species (72 of the 101) were numerically rare, and were represented by <20 colonies (Table 1).

--- SENTENCE 114 ---
Coral cover was high in Fagatele Bay (91.9% cover; Table 1).

--- SENTENCE 115 ---
However, the numerically abundant corals did not always have the highest percent cover.

--- SENTENCE 116 ---
For example, although *Galaxea fascicularis* was the most abundant species in Fagatele Bay, it covered <5% of the area surveyed.

--- SENTENCE 117 ---
Similarly *Pavona divaricata* covered <3% of the area surveyed despite being the third most abundant species.

--- SENTENCE 118 ---
In contrast, we recorded only 48 colonies of *Acropora hyacinthus*

--- SENTENCE 119 ---
but it covered >11% of the area surveyed.

--- SENTENCE 120 ---
*Porites rus* also covered >10% of the area surveyed, and the species *Acropora clathrata*, *Montipora grisea*, *Millepora* sp. and *Montipora efflorescens* each represented 5-10% of the coral cover in the bay (Table 1).

--- SENTENCE 121 ---
Total number of colonies and percent cover of each coral species recorded in Fagatele Bay and Pago Pago Harbour sites in 2004.

--- SENTENCE 168 ---
There were 20 species of coral recorded in Fagatele Bay in 2004 that had not been recorded in the bay previously.

--- SENTENCE 169 ---
Some of these represent new species records for the bay, although some of them are likely to represent differences in species identification between surveys, while others reflect changes in taxonomic nomenclature through the years.

--- SENTENCE 170 ---
We have used the nomenclature for species in American Samoa based on Paulay (1999) which synonomises many species that have been recorded previously in the area, as well as Veron (2000).

--- SENTENCE 171 ---
New species recorded in Fagatele Bay in 2004 are:

#### Family Acroporiidae

*Acropora abrotanoides* (may have been called *A*.

--- SENTENCE 172 ---
*irregularis* on previous surveys)

--- SENTENCE 173 ---
*Acropora austera*

*Acropora clathrata* (may have been called *A. tutuilensis* on previous surveys)

--- SENTENCE 174 ---
*Acropora intermedia* (previously *A*.

--- SENTENCE 175 ---
*Astreopora cucillata* 

*Montipora effusa* 

#### Family Agariciidae

*Coeloseris mayeri*

*Leptoseris incrustans*

#### Family Faviidae

*Cyphastrea micropthalma*

*Echinopora gemmacea* (probably pooled with *E. lamellosa* previously)

--- SENTENCE 176 ---
*Platygyra sinensis*

#### Family Merulinidae

*Merulina scabricula* (probably called *M. ampliata* on previous surveys)

#### Family Oculinidae

*Galaxea astreata*

#### Family Pectiniidae

*Oxypora lacera*

#### Family Poritiidae

*Alveopora spongiosa* (probably one of two *Alveopora* spp.

--- SENTENCE 177 ---
previously)

*Alveopora verilliana* (probably one of two *Alveopora* spp.

--- SENTENCE 178 ---
previously)

*Goniopora columna*

*Porites solida*

In addition to the new species recorded on the transects, we also noted two other species within the bay that had not been recorded in previous surveys: *Pachyseris rugosa* (Family Agariciidae) and *Oulophyllia bennettae* (Family Faviidae).

--- SENTENCE 179 ---
An updated species list for Fagatele Bay is included in Appendix 2.

--- SENTENCE 180 ---
At the family level, Fagatele Bay coral communities were dominated by Acroporiids (1511 colonies, 53.6% cover).

--- SENTENCE 181 ---
Oculinids (561 colonies, 4.77% cover), Agariciids (428 colonies; 4.91% cover), Poritiids (388 colonies, 12.23% cover), Faviids (146 colonies, 4.03% cover), and Pocilloporiids (224 colonies, 3.57% cover) were also an important component of the coral communities (Table 2).

--- SENTENCE 182 ---
The density of corals (number of colonies per square metre) varied across transects and depths (Table 3).

--- SENTENCE 183 ---
We recorded an average of 15.3 (±1.3SE) colonies/m<sup>2</sup> across all transects and depths.

--- SENTENCE 184 ---
The highest density (25.8 colonies/m<sup>2</sup>) was at 6m on Transect 3, although there were also 25.7 colonies/m<sup>2</sup> on the reef flat at Site B.

--- SENTENCE 185 ---
The lowest density was 4.1 colonies/m<sup>2</sup> at 12m on Transect 5 where the community was dominated by a few, very large, *Acropora hyacinthus* plates.

--- SENTENCE 186 ---
Coral cover recorded in Fagatele Bay in 2004 ranged from 31.1% at 9m on Transect 6 to 202.3% at 12m on Transect 5 (influenced by the presence of multiple very large and overlapping *Acropora hyacinthus* plates) (Table 4).

--- SENTENCE 187 ---
Note that due to the nature of the calculation of cover based on the average colony size for each size class, cover can be >100% in a complex 3D reef structure.

--- SENTENCE 188 ---
A full summary of all data from the 2004 survey of Fagatele Bay, including number of colonies in each size class and estimates of percent cover for each species, is provided in Appendix 3.

--- SENTENCE 189 ---

In general, the Fagatele Bay coral communities are dominated by small colonies (<20cm diameter).

--- SENTENCE 198 ---
Almost one third (29.7%) of corals in Fagatele Bay were in size class 2 (5-10cm), and only 1.4% of corals were >80cm in diameter (see Table 5).

--- SENTENCE 199 ---
In general recruitment (gauged as number of colonies <5cm diameter) was highest in shallower water at all sites.

#### *Trends in Fagatele Bay coral populations 2001-2004*

--- SENTENCE 200 ---
The reef flat communities in Fagatele Bay appear to have shown an improvement both in coral density and cover in the last three years.

--- SENTENCE 201 ---
Unfortunately only one reef flat site was surveyed in 2001 and it was in a slightly different area to the usual sites, so direct comparisons between the 2001 and 2004 surveys are difficult.

--- SENTENCE 202 ---
There has, however, been a notable increase in the number and cover of corals on the reef flat since the decline recorded in 1998 (Tables 3 & 4).

--- SENTENCE 203 ---
Due to the change in survey methods, the average size of colonies is not directly comparable between 2001 and 2004.

--- SENTENCE 204 ---
However, previous surveys have shown the mean colony diameter on the reef flat to range from 6-14cm (Birkeland et al 2004).

--- SENTENCE 205 ---
This is similar to our observations in 2004, whereby most colonies on the reef flat were <20cm, and the size classes 1 and 2 (<5cm and 5-10cm) were the most abundant (Table 5).

--- SENTENCE 206 ---
Coral communities on the reef slope generally showed an increase in coral cover (Table 4) from 2001 to 2004, consistent with the general trends seen in Fagatele Bay from 1995 (Birkeland et al. 2004).

--- SENTENCE 207 ---
We did notice a slight drop in coral cover at 9m at four of the six transects and at 3m on Transects 2 and 5, although the decline was not dramatic and most likely represents variation both in the areas sampled and method used between the two years.

--- SENTENCE 208 ---
Notably, however, there was a drop in cover from 100% to 37% at 12m on Transect 6, and from 190% to 90% at 12m on Transect 3 (Table 4).

--- SENTENCE 209 ---
Colony density varied considerably among years and sites.

--- SENTENCE 210 ---
Of the 20 transects surveyed, 11 showed a slight drop in coral density, while nine had increased slightly.

--- SENTENCE 211 ---
Again none of these changes were dramatic, and most likely represent variation in sampling site and method between the two years.

--- SENTENCE 212 ---
Comparison of average colony size on the reef slope in 2001 and 2004 is not possible due to the change in survey method.

--- SENTENCE 213 ---
However, in 2001, average colony diameter ranged from 10 to 26cm.

--- SENTENCE 214 ---
In 2004, our data, which shows 58% of corals were in the size range from 5-20cm, does not suggest there have been any major change in colony size in the bay since 2001.

#### *Fish in Fagatele Bay – 2004*

A total of 3090 fish belonging to 123 species were recorded during transect surveys in Fagatele Bay in 2004 (Table 6).

--- SENTENCE 258 ---
Of these, four were new species records for the transect surveys;

 *Odonus niger* (Family Balistidae) *Chaetodon lineolatus* (Family Chaetodontidae) *Halichores prosopeion* (Family Labridae) *

--- SENTENCE 259 ---
Chromis atripes* (Family Pomacentridae)

--- SENTENCE 260 ---
This brings the total number of species recorded in Fagatele Bay to 219 (see Appendix 5), although all four species that represent new records for Fagatele Bay have been recorded elsewhere in Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 261 ---
The fish communities on the reef slope in Fagatele Bay in 2004 were dominated by four species; two acanthurids - *Ctenochaetus striatus* (n=995) and *Acanthurus nigricans* (n=167), and two pomacentrids - *Plectroglyphidodon dickii* (n=176), and *Chromis iomelas* (n=151) (Table 6).

--- SENTENCE 262 ---
Other species that were common (n>80 individuals) included the caesionid *Caesio caerulaurea*, the pomacentrid *Chromis margaritifer*, and the acanthurid *Acanthurus triostegus* (Table 6).

--- SENTENCE 263 ---
Fish communities on the reef slope in Fagatele Bay as recorded in September, 2004.

#### *Trends in Fagatele Bay fish populations 2001-2004*

--- SENTENCE 447 ---
Overall there appears to have been a decline in fish populations in Fagatele Bay from 2001 to 2004.

--- SENTENCE 448 ---
We recorded less fish across the entire survey (3090 fish recorded in 24 reef slope transects in 2004 vs. 4456 fish recorded on the same 24 transects in 2001).

--- SENTENCE 449 ---
The average abundance of fish decreased at all depths since 2001 (3-18m; note the reef flat was not surveyed in 2004), although fish abundance remains the second highest recorded since 1988 (Figure 3).

--- SENTENCE 450 ---
The drop in abundance was least pronounced on the reef slope at 6m (an avarage of 161 fish/transect in 2004 compared to 172 fish/transect in 2001), and most dramatic on the reef slope at 9m (117 fish/transect in 2004 compared to 205 fish/transect in 2001)

--- SENTENCE 451 ---
Abundance of fish in Fagatele Bay through time.

--- SENTENCE 452 ---
Symbols refer to different sampling locations of the reef flat (●), and reef slope at 3m (■), 6m (▲), 9m (▼), 12m (♦) and 18m.

The decline in abundance appears to have occurred across most species and major family groups, although the relative abundance (% of the populations) of each family group has remained consistent between 2001 and 2004 (Table 7).

--- SENTENCE 453 ---
The largest decline was in acanthurids.

--- SENTENCE 454 ---
This was because the 2004 survey was conducted later in the year to avoid the very high recruitment pulse of this family (particularly *Ctenochaetus striatus*) that takes place early in the year (Green 2002), and which was recorded in two of the other surveys (1985 & 2001).

--- SENTENCE 455 ---
Therefore, the decline in the number of acanthurids was probably not due to a decline in adult populations, but rather the result of not recording the recruitment pulse for that year.

--- SENTENCE 456 ---
This is likely to be the case for some of the other families also, although the pattern is likely to be much less pronounced.

--- SENTENCE 457 ---
In contrast, we recorded considerably more Caesionids in 2004 than in 2001, although this probably reflects the transient and schooling nature of these species.

--- SENTENCE 458 ---
Changes in abundance of scarids may also reflect the schooling nature of some of these species.

--- SENTENCE 459 ---
Comparison of major fish families from 2001 and 2004 surveys of Fagatele Bay National Marine Sanctuary.

--- SENTENCE 460 ---
Total is the number of fish recorded in each year across all species within each family.

--- SENTENCE 461 ---
Proportion represents the percentage of all fish recorded in each year.

--- SENTENCE 462 ---
Data included is only from the reef slope transects (3-18m) in both years.

--- SENTENCE 463 ---
| 2001       |       | 2004       |
|----------------|-------|------------|-------|------------|
|                | Total | proportion | Total | proportion |
| ACANTHURIDAE   | 2083

--- SENTENCE 464 ---
| 46.7       | 1416  | 45.8       |
| CAESIONIDAE    | 31    | 0.7        | 133   | 4.3        |
| CHAETODONTIDAE | 255   | 5.7        | 197   | 6.4        |
| CIRRHITIDAE    | 52    | 1.2        | 29    | 0.9        |
| LABRIDAE

--- SENTENCE 465 ---
| 310   | 6.9        | 215   | 6.9        |

--- SENTENCE 466 ---
| POMACENTRIDAE  | 1284

--- SENTENCE 467 ---
| 28.8       | 764   | 24.7       |
| SCARIDAE       | 179   | 4.0        | 201   | 6.5        |
| SERRANIDAE     | 22    | 0.5        | 27    | 0.9        |

A decline in species richness was also apparent on the reef slope in Fagatele Bay from 2001 to 2004 (Figure 4).

--- SENTENCE 468 ---
The magnitude of the decline was similar at all depths (there were approximately four species fewer recorded per transect in 2004 compared with 2001).

--- SENTENCE 469 ---
There was a slightly smaller decline in species richness, relative to the other four depths, at 18m (the average number of species recorded per transect fell from 27 in 2001 to 25 in 2004).

--- SENTENCE 470 ---
Again species richness remains above the lowest levels recorded in 1988, and is very similar to that recorded in 1998 (Figure 4).

--- SENTENCE 471 ---
There were 5 families recorded in 2001 (Blennidae, Holocentridae, Microdesmidae, Ostracidae and Pempheridae) that were not recorded in 2004, although these were all uncommon in the 2001 surveys.

!

--- SENTENCE 472 ---
Species richness of fish in Fagatele Bay through time.

--- SENTENCE 473 ---
Symbols refer to different sampling locations of the reef flat (●), and reef slope at 3m (■), 6m (▲), 9m (▼), 12m (♦) and 18m.

For the four fish families that are important for the local fisheries, there were few major changes in the size structure of populations from 2001 to 2004 (Figure 5).

--- SENTENCE 474 ---
Notably, however, we did observe more large scarids in 2004 than in 2001.

--- SENTENCE 475 ---
This may be a sign that scarid populations are starting to recover from the effects of overfishing by the SCUBA fishery.

!

--- SENTENCE 476 ---
Size structure of populations of important fisheries species in Fagatele Bay.

--- SENTENCE 477 ---
The important fisheries species *Cephalopholis argus* was more abundant in Fagatele Bay in 2004 than in 2001, and we also observed more larger individuals (>30cm) in 2004 than in 2001 (Figure 6).

--- SENTENCE 478 ---
For *Ctenochaetus striatus* we observed fewer individuals in 2004 than in 2001, although this was probably related to the large number of small individuals (<7cm, recent recruits) that were recorded during the recruitment season in 2001 (Figure 6).

!

--- SENTENCE 479 ---
Abundance of important fisheries species in Fagatele Bay in 2004.

--- SENTENCE 480 ---
### *Benthic macroinvertebrates and fish habitat in Fagatele Bay*

Point count surveys of key macro invertebrates in Fagatele Bay showed that corals and macroalgae contributed most to the fish habitat across all sites and depths.

--- SENTENCE 481 ---
The proportion of points representing scleractinian corals ranged from 8% to 72% across all sites and depths (an average of 40%, most of which was encrusting, branching or massive coral), whereas 20% - 62.5% (mean = 46%) comprised macroalgae (most of which was pink coralline algae) (Table 8).

--- SENTENCE 482 ---
In general soft coral and sponges were the only common macro invertebrates other than scleractinian corals on the transects.

--- SENTENCE 483 ---
Non-living substrate made up, on average, less than 12% of the points counted with the highest proportion of 33% at 18m on Site 2 (Table 8).

--- SENTENCE 484 ---
The point count data collected during the 2004 survey of Fagatele Bay form a baseline for monitoring of fish habitat in future years.

--- SENTENCE 485 ---
Data on the abundance of benthic macroinvertebrates in Fagatele Bay, September 2004.

#### **Reefs Around Tutuila**

#### Corals in Pago Pago Harbour – 2004

A total of 330 corals colonies belonging to 35 species were recorded in the surveys at the two sites in Pago Pago Harbour in 2004.

--- SENTENCE 519 ---
The most common coral (132 colonies) was *Montipora grisea*.

--- SENTENCE 520 ---
Only four other species were abundant (represented by >20 colonies); *Pocillopora danae* (28 colonies) *Pocillopora damicornis* (22 colonies), *Montipora informis* (21 colonies) and *Pocillopora verrucosa* ((21 colonies).

--- SENTENCE 521 ---
All other species (n= 30) were rare (<11 colonies) (Table 1).

--- SENTENCE 522 ---
Overall coral cover in Pago Pago Harbour was only 22.3%, and only two species contributed substantially to the coral cover in the harbour.

--- SENTENCE 523 ---
*Montipora grisea* represented half of the total coral cover at the sites surveyed (11.3%; Table 1).

--- SENTENCE 524 ---
*Diploastrea heliopora* represented 4.9% of the area surveyed, although this was due to a single large colony on the 6m transect at Rainmaker.

--- SENTENCE 525 ---
*Pavona varians* covered 1.7% of the survey area, but all other species covered <0.7% (Table 1).

--- SENTENCE 526 ---
Overall coral cover was highest at 6m at Rainmaker (50.1%) and lowest at 6m at Aua (7.1%).

--- SENTENCE 527 ---
Coral cover at 3m depth was similar at both sites (12.8% at Rainmaker and 19.1% at Aua).

--- SENTENCE 528 ---
Coral density in Pago Pago Harbour was also low.

--- SENTENCE 529 ---
The highest density was at 3m depth at Aua (13.1 colonies/m²) and lowest at 6m depth at Aua (1.7 colonies/m²) (Table 9).

--- SENTENCE 530 ---
Cover was highest at 6m depth at Rainmaker (50.1%) and lowest at 6m at Aua (7.1%) (Table 10).

--- SENTENCE 531 ---
Overall, 34% of corals in Pago Pago Harbour were <5cm in diameter, and 92% of colonies were <20cm in diameter (Table 5).

--- SENTENCE 532 ---
There was only 1 large colony (>80cm) recorded in the harbour (*Diploastrea heliopora* at Rainmaker which has a diameter >10m) and this was undoubtably the reason for the high coral cover recorded at this site.

--- SENTENCE 533 ---
A full summary of all data from the 2004 survey of Pago Pago Harbour, including number of colonies in each size class and estimates of percent cover for each species, is provided in Appendix 4.

#### Trends in coral populations in Pago Pago Harbour 2001-2004

Coral populations at Rainmaker have shown signs of improvement from 2001 to 2004.

--- SENTENCE 534 ---
There has been a slight increase both in colony density and coral cover at 6m depth, and a more noticeable increase, particularly of cover, at 3m depth (Tables 9 & 10).

--- SENTENCE 535 ---
On the reef slope at Aua, however, there appears to have been a decline in the coral populations.

--- SENTENCE 536 ---
Coral density and percent cover at 3m in 2004 is almost half that recorded in 2001, and at 6m it is only about 20% of that recorded in 2001 (Tables 9 & 10).

#### *The Aua Transect*

As in previous years, the coral communities along the Aua Transect in 2004 were dominated by the coral species *Acropora nana*, *Pocillopora danae*, *Montipora grisea* (probably recorded as *Montipora verrilli* in previous surveys), and *Pavona divaricata* (Tables 11 & 12), although colony size was highly variable in each species (Table 13).

--- SENTENCE 551 ---
We did, however, record higher overall coral abundance and percent cover than in previous years (Table 14 & 15), and there is a trend indicating continued recovery of corals (i.e. increasing abundance and percent cover) at all distances from shore on the transect since 1998 (equivalent data prior to 1998 were not available for comparison in this report).

--- SENTENCE 552 ---
We recorded few non-coral invertebrate species along the transect, with *Stichopus chloronotus* being the most abundant (Table 16).

--- SENTENCE 637 ---

--- SENTENCE 638 ---
Average values per 30m transect have been included (Appendix 6), and can be used for more rigorous comparisons among years in future surveys.

--- SENTENCE 699 ---
| 174<br>34 | 398<br>50 |

Fish abundance decreased between 2001 and 2004 at the Sita Bay and Fagatele Bay sites, although remained constant between years at Cape Larsen (Figure 7).

--- SENTENCE 700 ---
Although we surveyed 10% less area at each of the sites (ie 90 metres total (3 x 30m) in 2004 vs 1 x 100m in 2001), the magnitude of the drop in fish abundance at Sita Bay and Fagatele Bay was more than would be expected based simply on sampling effort.

--- SENTENCE 701 ---
To account for the change in area surveyed in 2004, data from all years were converted to density (number of fish per hectare) for a more accurate comparison (Figure 8).

--- SENTENCE 702 ---
This result mirrors the abundance data based on transect size, but shows a slight increase in density of fish at Cape Larsen, and a less marked decrease in density of fish at the other two sites.

--- SENTENCE 703 ---
The change in sampling in 2004 to three replicate transects allows mean and error estimates to be calculated at each of the sites (Figure 8) which will allow more rigorous statistical comparisons with future survey results.

!

--- SENTENCE 704 ---
Abundance of fish (number of individuals per transect) through time at three sites around Tutuila based on data from the historic fish transects.

--- SENTENCE 705 ---
NB Data in 2004 represent the sum of three replicate 30m transects (90m total)

--- SENTENCE 706 ---
whereas data in other years is based on a single 100m transect.

Symbols represent each of the three sites; (●) Fagatele Bay, (■) Sita Bay and (▲) Cape Larsen.

--- SENTENCE 708 ---
Abundance of fish (standardised as number of individuals per hectare) through time at three sites around Tutuila based on data from the historic fish transects.

--- SENTENCE 709 ---
NB Data in 2004 represent the mean of three replicate 30m transects (±se) whereas data in other years is based on a single 100m transect.

--- SENTENCE 710 ---
Symbols represent each of the three sites; (●) Fagatele Bay, (■) Sita Bay and (▲) Cape Larsen.

--- SENTENCE 711 ---
Species richness was similar at all three sites in 2004, although it had declined slightly at Cape Larsen and Sita Bay, and remained constant at Fagatele Bay (Figure 9).

--- SENTENCE 712 ---
As noted in the Fagatele Bay survey results, the abundance and richness of the fish communities at the three historic fish sites remained higher than the lowest levels recorded in 1988 and in fact were very similar to levels recorded in the initial survey in 1977 (Figures 8 and 9).

!

--- SENTENCE 714 ---
Symbols represent each of the three sites; (●) Fagatele Bay, (■) Sita Bay and (▲) Cape Larsen.

--- SENTENCE 715 ---
The trends in fish abundance from 2001 to 2004 at the family level at the three historic sites mirrored the patterns seen in the Fagatele Bay survey.

--- SENTENCE 716 ---
We recorded a drop in abundance of acanthurids, chaetodontids, cirrhitids and pomacentrids at all three sites.

--- SENTENCE 717 ---
Labrids had declined at two of the three sites (Fagtele Bay and Sita Bay) but were considerably more abundant at Cape Larsen in 2004 than they had been in 2001.

--- SENTENCE 718 ---
Caesionids only occurred at Fagatele Bay, but were considerably more common in 2004 than they had been in 2001, as were serranids (although their abundance had changed little at the other two sites).

--- SENTENCE 719 ---
Interestingly, scarids had increased in abundance at all three sites (Figures 10 & 11).

!

--- SENTENCE 720 ---
Trends in fish abundance for common fish species for each of the three historic fish transect sites.

--- SENTENCE 721 ---
Data is based on total number of fish recorded along a 100m transect (1977-2001) and three replicate 30m transects (90m total) in 2004.

--- SENTENCE 722 ---
Symbols represent each of the three sites; (●) Fagatele Bay, (■) Sita Bay and (▲) Cape Larsen.

--- SENTENCE 723 ---

--- SENTENCE 724 ---

--- SENTENCE 725 ---

--- SENTENCE 726 ---
#### **DISCUSSION**

#### **Fagatele Bay National Marine Sanctuary**

#### *Coral Communities in Fagatele Bay*

The surveys of coral communities in Fagatele Bay National Marine Sanctuary in September 2004 indicate continued improvement in the condition of the reefs within the bay.

--- SENTENCE 727 ---
Coral abundance and cover had increased at most sites and depths in the bay from 2001 to 2004.

--- SENTENCE 728 ---
There was an apparent decline in coral communities at some depths and at some sites, although overall there was no evidence of major declines across the bay, and fluctuations in coral cover and abundance most likely reflect natural levels of variation.

--- SENTENCE 729 ---
Importantly, there was no evidence that the coral communities at Fagatele Bay had been affected by Hurricane Heta, which hit Tutuila on 5 January 2004.

--- SENTENCE 730 ---
This is probably not surprising given the main impacts from the hurricane were focused on the northern side of the island (N. Daschbach and P. Craig, pers comm.).

--- SENTENCE 731 ---
Coral communities on the reef slope in Fagatele Bay remain dominated by Acroporiids (especially *Montipora* spp.)

--- SENTENCE 732 ---
and the fast-growing *Porites rus*.

--- SENTENCE 733 ---
*Galaxea fasicularis* also continues to be one of the most dominant coral species in the bay, which most likely reflects its ability to fragment and spread.

--- SENTENCE 734 ---
The reef flat communities appear to be recovering following the major die-off in 1998 associated with an extreme low-tide event (see Birkeland et al. 2004).

--- SENTENCE 735 ---
There is evidence of an increase in coral abundance and cover at the two reef flat sites since the 1998 surveys, and coral communities appear to be approaching levels of abundance and cover equivalent to those recorded in 1988.

--- SENTENCE 736 ---
The change in sampling method for corals, from the point-quarter method to belt transects, appears to have been effective.

--- SENTENCE 737 ---
In a similar period of time, we were able to sample almost three times as many coral colonies in the bay as had been sampled in previous years.

--- SENTENCE 738 ---
As a result we recorded at least eleven new coral species in Fagatele Bay, taking into account variation in coral identification between years.

--- SENTENCE 739 ---
The main limitation to the belt-transect method used in the 2004 surveys was the constraint in measuring the exact size of each coral colony.

--- SENTENCE 740 ---
As corals were only categorised into size-classes, it is not possible to directly compare the average size of colonies in the bay among years.

--- SENTENCE 741 ---
However, from the distribution of colonies within the seven different size classes, it is evident that most colonies within Fagatele Bay are small (<20cm in diameter) with only a few reaching sizes >40cm diameter.

--- SENTENCE 742 ---
This is consistent with the range of average sizes of colonies recorded in the 2001 surveys (average colony diameter 7cm – 26.6cm, Birkeland et al. 2004).

--- SENTENCE 743 ---
However, there are some spectacular exceptions to this rule (see below).

--- SENTENCE 744 ---
The belt-transect survey method instigated in 2004 to survey corals in Fagatele Bay resulted in a much more comprehensive population sample, although even using this method, it was apparent that some of the key coral colonies within the bay were not being represented in the surveys.

--- SENTENCE 745 ---
In an attempt to record the presence and highlight the conservation value of these key coral

colonies, we have documented details of the corals below as key conservation features of Fagatele Bay.

#### • Dynamite Bommie

This is a very large colonie of *Porites lutea* that exceeds 10m in diameter.

--- SENTENCE 746 ---
It occurs in relatively shallow water near the 6m transect along Site 4.

--- SENTENCE 747 ---
The GPS coordinates of the bommie are in Appendix 1.

--- SENTENCE 748 ---
This colony was damaged by a dynamite blast just prior to the 2001 survey (see Birkeland et al. 2004).

--- SENTENCE 749 ---
In the vicinity of the dynamite bommie (towards and on Site 3) are at least six other *Porites* sp. coral heads larger than 1m in diameter.

--- SENTENCE 750 ---
All these colonies are likely to be many hundreds of years old and importantly they are a key habitat feature for reef fish in shallow water.

--- SENTENCE 751 ---
Large schools of parrotfish, emperors and snappers have been observed in the vicinity of these coral heads.

--- SENTENCE 752 ---
# • Mono-specific stands

In the reef area immediately west of Site 4 are a number of large monospecific stands of coral.

--- SENTENCE 753 ---
In the deeper reef area there is an extensive area of *Pachyseris rugosa*.

--- SENTENCE 754 ---
At its shallowest point it is in 15m of water, and continues to below 30m depth.

--- SENTENCE 755 ---
The diameter of the stand is in excess of 50m. Many of the individual *P. rugosa* corals within this mono-specific stand are also very large relative to the normal size of corals of this species.

--- SENTENCE 756 ---
This is the largest stand of this species ever seen by the authors, with a collective experience of 60 years of field research on coral reefs.

--- SENTENCE 757 ---
The time frame required to achieve local dominance of this magnitude by such a relatively slow growing species would be many decades or, more likely, centuries.

--- SENTENCE 758 ---
Further up the slope west of Site 4 and in shallower water above the *P. rugosa* stand are large areas (>10m diameter) of *Porites rus*, *Hydnophora rigida* and *Echinorpora gemmacea*.

--- SENTENCE 759 ---
There are also several unusually large (> 2m diameter) specimens of *Merulina ampliata* between the mono-specific stands mentioned above.

--- SENTENCE 760 ---
Unfortunately there was evidence of considerable recent anchor damage in the *H. rigida* stand, which may compromise its conservation value.

--- SENTENCE 761 ---
In the area even further west of Site 4 and towards Site 5, at approximately 6-10m depth, the reef is dominated by large plates of *Acropora hyacinthus*.

--- SENTENCE 762 ---
The coral cover approaches 100% due to the high density of large, overlapping plates.

--- SENTENCE 763 ---
The occurrence of so many large mono-specific stands of coral in the general vicinity of Sites 4 and 5 suggests the area may well have interesting hydrodynamic or biological qualities that promote the dominance of a few species, and which facilitate colonies persisting through time and attaining large size.

--- SENTENCE 764 ---
Previous surveys (Birkeland et al. 1987, 1996, 2003, 2004; Green et al 1999) noted a large stand (>6m diameter) of *Echinopora hirsuitissima* at 9m at Site 2.

--- SENTENCE 765 ---
They suggested the coral had survived the 1979 outbreak of *Acanthaster planci* as it was not a favoured prey species of the starfish.

--- SENTENCE 766 ---
It also survived the hurricanes in the early 1990s, and by 1995 was one of few areas of high coral cover in the bay.

--- SENTENCE 767 ---
Unfortunately, we found that this colony was largely dead in the 2004 surveys, although small patches of tissue remained alive and may be enough to ensure the continued persistence of the colony.

--- SENTENCE 768 ---
Future surveys targeted at monitoring the coral communities within Fagatele Bay should incorporate some measure of the persistence or condition of these key conservation features.

--- SENTENCE 769 ---
Possible options include recording detail of the locations (based on GPS coordinates), size or extent, and overall condition of the colonies.

--- SENTENCE 770 ---
*Fish communities in Fagatele Bay*

The fish communities in Fagatele Bay showed a slight reduction in abundance at all sites and all depths from 2001 to 2004.

--- SENTENCE 771 ---
The drop is not dramatic and has occurred across most species and families.

--- SENTENCE 772 ---
The decline most likely reflects seasonal variation in fish abundance: the 2004 surveys were done in September (late winter - early spring) whereas previous surveys were done in the summer recruitment season.

--- SENTENCE 773 ---
The drop in fish abundance at other sites around Tutuila is consistent with a seasonal effect, although there may also have been a population decline associated with impacts on the coral communities by Hurricane Heta.

--- SENTENCE 774 ---
The fact that corals in Fagatele Bay showed no evidence of hurricane damage makes this latter explanation unlikely.

--- SENTENCE 775 ---
Importantly, there is evidence from the 2004 surveys that parrotfish (Family Scaridae) are increasing in abundance and size in Fagatele Bay.

--- SENTENCE 776 ---
This may be the first sign that parrotfish populations are starting to recover from the destructive SCUBA fishery which was banned in 2001 and was particularly damaging to these fishes (Page 1998; Green 2002).

--- SENTENCE 777 ---
However, despite this encouraging sign, the coral reef fish populations of Fagatele Bay still show signs of overfishing.

--- SENTENCE 778 ---
Several large species that are characteristic of unfished reefs in the Indo-Pacific remain conspicuous by their absence or small size in the Sanctuary.

--- SENTENCE 779 ---
These include species such as maori wrasse (*Cheilinus undulatus*), sharks, and larger species of serranids and scarids, all of which are know to be particularly vulnerable to fishing (Green 2002).

--- SENTENCE 780 ---
Given that many of these species are more abundant elsewhere in Samoa (Green 2002), these results indicate that Fagatele Bay is still feeling the impacts of overfishing.

--- SENTENCE 781 ---
Coral reef fish assemblages can take >5 years to start to show the benefits of improved fisheries management.

--- SENTENCE 782 ---
Therefore, if fishing has declined on the reefs in the Sanctuary, we would expect to see increased abundance and biomass of key fisheries species in the Fagatele Bay by the next survey (due in 2007).

--- SENTENCE 783 ---
Incorporating measures of biomass in future surveys would be especially valuable in monitoring important fisheries species

#### **Reefs around Tutuila**

#### *Coral communities in Pago Pago Harbour (including the Aua Transect)*

Coral populations at Rainmaker continue to show signs of improvement, with coral cover and abundance increasing between the 2001 and 2004 surveys.

--- SENTENCE 784 ---
Notably we are starting to see quite a few recruits in the area, especially in shallow water, which is indicative that the reefs are showing signs of recovery following management measures to reduce pollution in the harbour (Green 2002; Peshut 2003).

--- SENTENCE 785 ---
There were 50 small colonies (<5cm diameter) recorded at 3m depth at Rainmaker, and five of these were juvenile Acroporids.

--- SENTENCE 786 ---
Coral communities on the reef flat along the Aua Transect are also showing continued signs of improvement.

--- SENTENCE 787 ---
While coral diversity remained relatively low, the abundance and percent cover of corals has increased dramatically from 1999 to 2004.

--- SENTENCE 788 ---
This may be related to the removal of the

wrecks on the reef flat near the Aua Transect (see Birkeland and Belliaveau 2000), but may also reflect the natural ability of populations of the common coral species to rapidly expand i.e. *Acropora nana* easily fragments facilitating the rapid formation of new colonies.

--- SENTENCE 789 ---
Surprisingly, however, coral populations on the reef slope at Aua appear to have declined dramatically to less than half that recorded in 2001.

--- SENTENCE 790 ---
The reef slope populations are unlikely to have been affected by either by Hurricane Heta (which struck the northern side of Tutuila, and is therefore unlikely to have affected the harbour) or the removal of the wreck from the adjacent reef flat (see Birkeland and Belliaveau 2000).

--- SENTENCE 791 ---
There appears to be no logical explanation for this sudden decline, especially since the reef flat populations in the same area appear to be doing well.

--- SENTENCE 792 ---
Overall these results from the 2004 surveys of coral communities in Pago Pago Harbour suggest that the reefs are starting to show signs of recovery following management measures to reduce pollution in the harbour (Green 2002; Peshut 2003), although the decline on the slope at Aua may be cause for concern and it is important that the corals at this site continue to be monitored.

#### *Fish communities around Tutuila (Historic Fish Transects)*

Fish abundance and species richness has dropped slightly on the three historic fish transects to levels similar to those recorded in 1998.

--- SENTENCE 793 ---
The decline may be associated with overfishing, Hurricane Heta (especially at Cape Larsen and Sita Bay which are on the northern side of Tutuila Island – although this is unlikely to explain declines in Fagatele Bay on the southern side), or may reflect the slight change in survey method (we surveyed 10m less in 2004 than in previous years).

--- SENTENCE 794 ---
However, the most likely explanation for the drop in fish abundance is seasonal variation since surveys were conducted in the early spring (September) of 2004, rather than late summer (March) as in previous years.

--- SENTENCE 795 ---
Certainly the fish populations remain similar to those recorded in 1977 which suggests there is little cause for alarm, although it will be important that these sites continue to be monitored in the future, preferably in late winter to avoid the recruitment season.

--- SENTENCE 796 ---
It will also be necessary to continue to monitor fish size to compare counts from different years and seasons (to understand the influence of recruitment pulses in previous surveys).

#### **CONCLUSIONS AND RECOMMENDATIONS**

- Coral populations in Fagatele Bay appear relatively healthy.

--- SENTENCE 797 ---
There is evidence of continued but slow recovery of coral communities through time.

--- SENTENCE 798 ---
- Coral populations on the reef slope in Pago Pago Harbour are also showing signs of recovery.

--- SENTENCE 799 ---
There is evidence of new coral recruitment at Rainmaker, although there has been an unexplained decline in coral populations on the reef slope at Aua.
-

--- SENTENCE 800 ---
The corals along the reef flat on the Aua Transect are also continuing to recover, possibly as a result of improved management to reduce pollution in the harbour.
-

--- SENTENCE 801 ---
The slight drop in fish populations recorded in Fagatele Bay and at the three historic fish transects in the 2004 surveys (compared with levels recorded in 2001) is most likely associated with seasonal variation in populations.

--- SENTENCE 802 ---
Fish populations remain well above the lowest levels recorded in 1988.

--- SENTENCE 803 ---
- An increase in size and abundance of parrotfish (Family Scaridae) may be the first evidence that fish populations in Fagatele Bay are recovering from overfishing, and that the ban on SCUBA fishing has been successful.

--- SENTENCE 804 ---
However, the absence of key large reef fish species indicates there are still effects of fishing in the Sanctuary.

--- SENTENCE 805 ---
- Switching to belt-transects for the coral surveys in Fagatele Bay and Pago Pago Harbour proved successful.

--- SENTENCE 806 ---
We were able to sample many more corals in a similar period of time.

--- SENTENCE 807 ---
We recommend this approach continue to be used for the Fagatele surveys and to facilitate comparison with the Archipelago-wide surveys.
- Several mono-specific stands of scleractinian corals in the centre of Fagatele Bay were identified.

--- SENTENCE 808 ---
These have high conservation value and require ongoing monitoring.

--- SENTENCE 809 ---
- We recommend future surveys be conducted in September to avoid the summer recruitment season for reef fish.

--- SENTENCE 810 ---
In doing so, it is important that the survey methods continue to estimate the size of each fish.

--- SENTENCE 811 ---
This will allow for an interpretation of the data based on size structure, which should be possible back to 1998 when sizes were first recorded (but have not been analysed to date).

--- SENTENCE 812 ---
Alternatively, juveniles can be removed from the dataset to allow for a comparison of adult populations among surveys.

--- SENTENCE 813 ---
Sizes can also be used to monitor fisheries biomass in the Sanctuary in the long term.
